Treatment with the SQ-standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet in children above and below the age of 10 - Efficacy, safety and immunological response; Purpose: Treatment with the SQ-standardised grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), GRAZAX® (Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK, Denmark), is efficacious and well-tolerated in grass allergic patients. Younger children are often perceived as good candidates for immunotherapy, and post-hoc analyses to explore possible differences between younger and older children were performed for a grass AIT paediatric phase III trial. Methods: Of 253 children aged 5-16 years with grass allergy were randomised 1:1 to receive grass AIT or placebo prior to and during the grass pollen season 2007. Symptomatic medications were provided as rescue medication to both groups. The population was divided into two age groups comprising 140 children aged 5-10 years and 113 children aged 11-16 years. The effect of age on efficacy was also analysed by including age as a covariate in the symptom score analysis. Results: Analysis of the difference between active and placebo for each age group showed a slightly higher relative difference in symptom score for the younger children compared with the older, 26% versus 19%. The difference between active and placebo was statistically significant for the 5-10 year-olds (P = 0.047), although the trial was powered for analysis of the population as a whole. Including age as a covariate in the symptom score analysis indicated age to have no influence on the efficacy, as demonstrated by a p-value of 0.6 for the interaction between age and treatment. In the AIT group the percentage of adverse events assessed as treatment-related was nominally lower for the younger group than for the older, 32% versus 40%. The percentages of adverse events assessed as moderate or severe in the active group were similar for the two age groups. For both active and placebo, the overall number of adverse events was higher in the younger group than in the group of older children. The immunological response (IgE, IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor) was similar for the two age groups. There was a tendency for the younger group towards an increased response in IgE-blocking factor at the onset of the grass pollen season, both in the active and the placebo group. Conclusions: The phase III trial, investigating the SQ-standardised grass AIT, demonstrated once-daily administration to be clinically efficacious and well-tolerated in children between 5 and 16 years. Comparison of two age groups revealed similar efficacy and tolerability after treatment with grass AIT independent of age.
SQ-standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet in children induces clinical and immunological responses similar to those seen in adults; Background: Clinical results and serum antibody responses were investigated after treatment with the grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), GRAZAX® (Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK, Denmark) in children and results compared to those obtained after the same treatment in adults. Purpose: In randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled multi-centre trials in children (5-16 years) and adults (18-65 years), AIT were initiated prior to the grass pollen season, and once-daily treatment was continued until the end of the season. All participants had a clinical history of moderate to severe grass allergy. The daily rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medications scores during the grass pollen season and the levels of Phleum pratense (Table presented) specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor (i.e. serum components competing with IgE for allergen binding) in serum samples before, during and after the season were assessed. Results: Statistically significant improvements were found for the rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores in the grass AIT group relative to placebo. The improvements were comparable to those seen for adults with similar grass pollen exposure (see Table 1). In children, specific IgG4 was significantly increased from the initial level at all assessments after treatment initiation in the active group (P < 0.001). The increase after 7.3 months was 32 U/L. In adults, the corresponding increase was 45.7 U/L (P < 0.001). The IgE-blocking factor increased in the grass AIT groups in both children and adults (P < 0.001 in both trials). In children the difference from the initial level in the active group was 0.25 after 7.3 months, while in adults the difference was 0.15 after 8 months. Conclusions: SQ-standardised grass AIT treatment is effective in children with grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis. Improvements in efficacy scores and immunological responses were similar to those in a comparable trial in adults, in whom a disease modifying effect was shown in terms of clinically relevant benefits that were sustained for at least one year after 3 years of treatment.

Sustained, disease-modifying effect of grass allergy tablet immunotherapy 1 year after a 3-year treatment period; Background: The sustained efficacy with the SQ-standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax® (Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T, ALK-Abello, Denmark), 1 year after a 3-year treatment period, was investigated in a randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled, phase III trial. Participants were adults with a history of moderate to severe grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis inadequately controlled by symptomatic medications. Methods: The full analysis set included 283 participants (157 AIT, 126 placebo). All participants had free access to symptomatic medications during the grass pollen season. Primary and key secondary efficacy endpoints were rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores, quality of life, and percentages of symptom and medication free days. Immunological endpoints included specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor. Safety was assessed by adverse events. Results: 1 year after treatment, the grass AIT group showed a 26% reduction in mean rhinoconjunctivitis symptom scores (P < 0.001) and a 29% reduction in medication scores (P = 0.022) when compared with placebo. The corresponding median reductions were 31% and 52%. This level of efficacy was similar to the efficacy during treatment. The mean difference in percentages of symptom and medication free days during the entire season was 27% (P= 0.038) (median difference 49%) in favour of AIT. The grass AIT group also reported better rhinoconjunctivitis quality of life (P= 0.004). No safety issues were identified. Conclusion: Significant and clinically relevant efficacy of grass AIT was sustained 1 year after a 3-year treatment period. This is the first time evidence of disease modification by sublingual tablet immunotherapy has been demonstrated in a large-scale, randomised, placebo-controlled trial.
A contemporary review of sublingual immunotherapy; The sublingual route has become an interesting and novel therapeutic option for the immunotherapeutic management of patients with allergies. Immunotherapy modifies the immune response by decreasing the specific IgE levels and Th2-type inflammation in the mucosa when allergen exposure occurs, shifting this toward a Th1-type response. Credible evidence exists of both effectiveness and safety of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) from several placebo-controlled double-blind studies. SLIT has been shown to be an effective treatment of allergic rhinitis and asthma in both children and adults. The therapy is well tolerated with mainly minor gastrointestinal side effects that subside in few weeks. The ideal treatment length and dosage still require further verification. Additional studies evaluating long-term efficacy and the immune response of SLIT still need to be performed, and additional standardized antigens still need to be developed. © 2009 The American Laryngological, Rhinological and Otological Society, Inc.

The SQ-standardized grass allergy immunotherapy tabletis well tolerated over 3 years of treatment; Introduction: Traditionally, allergen-specific immunotherapy has been administered via subcutaneous injections or sublingual drops. Recently, a new formulation administered sublingually, allergy immunotherapy tablets (AIT), has emerged. Many large double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled trials have demonstrated AIT to be well tolerated and efficacious in reducing rhinoconjunctivitis symptoms and the use of rescue medications. We investigated the nature, severity and duration of the most common adverse events (AEs) associated with treatment with an SQ-standardized grass AIT (Phleum pratense, 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK, Denmark) during a trial conducted over a 3 year period. Methods: Subjects with grass-allergy (n=634) were enrolled in a double-blind, placebo-controlled, multinational trial, of which 351 continued in a 2-year extension. Approval was obtained from the local IRBs and written informed consent obtained from all subjects. Subjects were randomized 1:1 to grass AIT or placebo. All AEs were collected and rated as mild, moderate or severe. AEs were assessed as treatment-emergent if the start date was on or after the date of the first tablet intake. Reactions occurring for a limited period of time each day on consecutive days were reported as one event. Results: The most frequent AEs after grass AIT were oral pruritus (164 subjects, 191 events), mouth edema (65 subjects, 70 events), ear pruritus (41 subjects, 42 events) and throat irritation (34 subjects, 37 events). The majority of these AEs were mild (84%) or moderate (13%) and transient of nature. There was a sharp decrease in the percentage of subjects reporting these AEs within the first weeks ofthe trial (figure). Approximately 10% continued to have oral itching during the entire trial, typically lasting 10 minutes after tablet administration. Withdrawal due to these local AEs was low (n=6, 1%). Conclusions: The most frequent AEs after grass AIT were local reactions affecting the mucosal membranes in mouth, ear and throat. The majority of AEs were mild and generally subsided shortly after treatment initiation, indicating development of tolerance. A small proportion of the subjects experienced local AEs daily upon tablet administration. However, the rate of withdrawals due to local AEs was low, indicating that grass AIT was well tolerated and these side effects were acceptable to most patients. (Figure Presented).
The future of sublingual immunotherapy.; Sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) is currently the most prescribed form of allergen immunotherapy in many European countries. Its use has been accepted in the international consensus publications, and recently also the scepticism of USA scientists is attenuated. Still, this treatment may be improved, and the possible developments consist of modification of the materials, use of adjuvants and use of recombinant allergens. Moreover, new applications of SLIT, such as food allergy, seem promising. Concerning materials, the future form of SLIT is likely to be represented by tablets, which were already tested for efficacy and safety with grass pollen extracts, and are likely to increase the convenience for the patient by the use of no-updosing schedule. Adjuvants fitting with the characteristics of SLIT seem to be CpG oligodeoxynucleotides (CpG), able to interact with the Toll-like receptor 9 (TLR9) whose activation induces a Th1-like pattern of cytokine release, combination of 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D3 plus dexamethasone (VitD3-Dex), and Lactobacillus plantarum. The approach with recombinant allergens, named component-resolved diagnosis, offers the possibility to tailor immunotherapy, which was found to be effective in two randomized trials of subcutaneous SIT (16-17), while studies with SLIT are not yet available. Regarding food allergy, an important controlled study demonstrated that SLIT with hazelnut is able to increase patients tolerance over possible reactions from inadvertent assumption of the culprit food, and warrants for further trials with other foods.

RESULTS: The use of Grazax plus symptomatic medications compared to standard care showed an improvement in patient outcomes
Efficacy of grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy for three consecutive seasons and after cessation of treatment: The ECRIT study; Background: Data supporting a carry-over effect with sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) are scarce. This randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study evaluated the efficacy, carry-over effect and safety of grass pollen SLIT using co-seasonal treatment. Methods: Patients (7.9-64.7 years) with grass pollen allergy received ultra-rush titration with increasing doses (30, 90, 150 and 300 IR) of a 5-grass pollen mixture every 20 min at the start of the pollen seasons, followed by 300 IR daily until the end of the pollen seasons. A baseline season (no SLIT) was followed by three consecutive treatment seasons and one follow-up season. Symptoms, medication and adverse events were documented and specific immunoglobulin (Ig)E and IgG4 measured. Results: Data were analysed for 183 of the 213 randomized patients. Mean treatment duration varied between seasons (81.8-92.7 days). Combined scores (symptoms and medication) improved progressively across treatment seasons (up to 44.7% improvement for SLIT compared with baseline) and fluctuated between -11.3% and -14.8% for placebo (P < 0.05). Similar changes were observed for symptom scores, with a successive decrease of 39.7% (SLIT) and fluctuations between +13.6% and -1.51% for placebo (P < 0.05). Combined score (P = 0.0508) and symptom score improvements (P = 0.0144) with SLIT continued during follow up. Increases in specific IgG4 observed in the first season were sustained for SLIT vs placebo throughout treatment (P = 0.0001). Titration and daily SLIT were well tolerated. No serious systemic or anaphylactic reactions were reported. Conclusions: Seasonal SLIT with ultra-rush titration is well tolerated and effective from the first treatment season onwards. These data indicate a carry-over effect of seasonal SLIT. © 2008 The Authors.

Administration regimens for sublingual immunotherapy to pollen allergens: What do we know?; The modalities of administration of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT), including dosing, build-up phase, duration of the treatment, and frequency of the maintenance dose are largely variable. In the case of pollen (SLIT), the complexity increases, since preseasonal, coseasonal and pre-coseasonal regimens can be used. The administration regimens are of relevance from a practical point of view, but can also have economic implications. We review herein the available literature (randomized double blind controlled studies) on pollen SLIT, in order to derive experimentally-supported suggestions about the regimens of administration that should be preferred. © 2009 John Wiley & Sons A/S.
Allergen-specific immunotherapy in allergic rhinitis and asthma. Mechanisms and proof of efficacy; Appeared at the beginning of the 20th century, allergen-specific immunotherapy (SIT) has long been used in allergic rhinitis and asthma without any knowledge of its mechanisms of action or any tangible proof of its efficacy. However, from the beginning of the era of evidence-based medicine, a number of placebo-controlled studies have been published and reached a sufficient number to assess the cellular events induced by SIT and allow meta-analysis to provide guidelines based on proofs. Controlled studies and meta-analysis concerned not only subcutaneous immunotherapy but also the sublingual route, demonstrating an effect of SIT on symptoms and medication use. Most recently sublingual tablets were proposed in allergic rhinitis. This paper reviews the mechanisms of SIT, the evidence of efficacy of SIT from the injective to the sublingual route and reminds the current guidelines. © 2009 Elsevier Ltd. All rights reserved.
Foxp3 is increased in the sublingual mucosa after grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy and is accompanied by increases in serum IgG4 and serum inhibitory activity for allergen-IgE binding to B cells; Background: Sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) using an SQ-standardised extract of Phleum Pratense (ALK Abello, Horsholm Denmark, daily drops containing 25 μg Phleum p5 daily for 12-18 months) resulted in improvements in patients' overall assessment of hayfever severity, suppression of the late skin response and an increase in serum IgG4 level, but no change in T cell numbers, CD1a+ Langerhans' cells or IL-12 mRNA expression in the sublingual mucosa (Lima M, Clin Exp Allergy 2002). We now report measurements of dendritic cell subsets, mast cells, T cells and Foxp3 as a marker of T regulatory cells in the sublingual mucosa and serum inhibitory activity for allergen-IgE binding to B cells (IgE-FAB). Methods: Cryostat/paraformaldhyde sections from the sublingual mucosa from 14 SLIT-treated, nine placebo treated and eight normal controls were immunostained for myeloid dendritic cells (CD1c), plasmacytoid dendritic cells (CD303), mast cells (AA1) and T cells (CD3) using dual-immunofluorescence microscopy. Sections from approximately six subjects from each group were also immunostained for Foxp3 and IL-9. Positive cells were counted blind and expressed per mm2 in the epithelium and subepithelium. Serum inhibitory activity for IgE-FAB was measured at different time points. Results: Epithelial myeloid dendritic cell (mDC) numbers were similar in each group. There were greater subepithelial mDCs in placebo treated patients than normal controls (P = 0.05). Few plasmacytoid dendritic (pDC) cells were seen in any group and were largely confined to the subepithelium. There were possibly fewer subepithelial mast cells and more epithelial T cells in SLIT-compared to placebo-treated subjects (P = 0.1 for both). Foxp3+ cells within the epithelium were present in five of seven SLIT-treated patients compared to zero of six placebo-treated patients (P = 0.014, Chi-Square test) and Foxp3 co-localised to T cells in two subjects in whom dual immunofluorescence was informative. There was a time-dependent increase in serum inhibitory activity for IgE-FAB (P < 0.001), maximal at 6 weeks when compared to placebo-treated patients. Conclusion: These data suggest that SLIT may operate through similar mechanisms to SCIT and is associated with recruitment of T cells including possible Foxp3+ Tregs to the sublingual epithelium, a possible reduction in subepithelial mast cells and is accompanied by increases in serum IgG4 and IgG-associated inhibition of IgE-facilitated allergen presentation.

Sustained, disease-modifying effect of grass allergen tablet immunotherapy 1 year after completion of treatment; Background: The sustained efficacy following withdrawal of ALK grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK-Abello, Hørsholm, Denmark) was investigated in a randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled Phase III trial. Participants were adults with a clinical history of moderate to severe grass pollen allergy inadequately controlled by the use of symptomatic medication. Methods: The full analysis set included 283 participants (157 active, 126 placebo) who entered the follow-up investigation 1 year after completion of 3 years treatment. Efficacy endpoints comprised rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores, quality of life, and percentages of symptom and medication free days. Immunological endpoints included serum levels of grass pollen specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor. Safety was assessed by means of adverse events. Results: One year after completion of treatment the active group showed a 26% reduction in rhinoconjunctivitis symptom scores (P < 0.001) and a 29% reduction in medication scores (P < 0.02) when compared with placebo over the entire grass pollen season. This level of sustained efficacy between active and placebo groups was not significantly different compared to the previous season, i.e. during treatment. The difference in percentages of symptom and medication free days during the entire season was 27% (P = 0.04) in favour of AIT. Participants in the active group also reported better rhinoconjunctivitis quality of life both during the entire grass pollen season and during the peak season. Sustained clinical benefit after treatment completion was accompanied by persistent immunological changes as observed for subcutaneous immunotherapy. Six mild adverse events were assessed as treatmentrelated. Eight serious events (four in each group) were all assessed as unlikely related to treatment. Conclusion: This double-blind analysis 1 year after completion of a 3-year period of daily all year round treatment showed significant effects on the primary and key secondary endpoints, thereby demonstrating sustained efficacy with ALK grass AIT following completion of treatment. This is the first time that evidence of disease modification by allergen tablet immunotherapy has been shown in a large-scale, randomised, placebo-controlled trial. No safety issues were identified.
Efficacy and safety of a 300IR five-grass pollen SLIT tablet in children and adolescents with grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis; Background: The efficacy and safety of a five-grass pollen 300IR sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) tablet (Stallergenes SA, France) has recently been confirmed in children and adolescents with grass pollen allergic rhinoconjunctivitis. Secondary and complementary analyses on the trial's intention-to-treat (ITT) population were performed for the Average Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (ARTSS), the six Average Individual Symptom Scores (AISSs) and for safety parameters according to age class (5 to 11 and 12 to 17). Methods: Two hundred and seventy-eight children (5-17 years of age) with grass pollen-related rhinoconjunctivitis were included in a multinational, randomized, doubleblind, placebo-controlled phase III trial. They were treated with once-daily five grasspollen SLIT tablets or placebo tablets 4 months prior to and then throughout the pollen season. Each symptom was evaluated daily by the patients (with parental assistance, for the youngest) on a four-point scale from 0 (absent) to 3 (severe). Results: The difference between 300IR and placebo tablets was significant for rhinorrhea (P = 0.038), nasal congestion (P = 0.0012), ocular pruritus (P < 0.0001) and watery eyes (P = 0.0008). Improvements in sneezing and nasal pruritus were not statistically significant. Patients aged 12 to 17 had higher ARTSSs and AISSs than those aged 5 to 11 years whatever the treatment group and the analysed period (pollen season or pollen peak). In both age classes (12-17 and 5-11), the ARTSS was lower in the 300IR group (3.77 and 2.94, respectively) than in the placebo group (4.98 and 4.08, respectively). A complementary subgroup analysis on safety parameters revealed a similar safety profile of the 300IR five-grass pollen sublingual tablet in age subgroups 5-11 and 12-17, as well as in sub-goups 5-7 and 8-11. Conclusion: Five-grass pollen tablet immunotherapy has a significant effect on eye and nasal symptoms such as nasal congestion and rhinorrhea and was in this study an effective treatment for grass polleninduced allergic rhinoconjunctivitis in children and adolescents, independently of age class. Also, the same dose can be administered to allergic children and adolescents aged 5-17 years without any influence on the tolerability of the treatment.
Early onset of action of a five-grass pollen 300 IR sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) tablet evaluated in an allergen Challenge Chamber; Background: The efficacy and safety of the 300 IR grass pollen SLIT tablet have been evaluated in clinical studies performed during the pollen season. However, no information on the onset of action of any type of SLIT has been reported. The Vienna Challenge Chamber (VCC) was developed as a pharmacodynamic assessment tool in which highly reproducible allergen challenge sessions are used to overcome problems related to pollen season unpredictability. Methods: Patients with grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis were randomized into either active (300 IR SLIT tablets, od) (Oralair® Grasses Stallergenes, France) or placebo groups treated for 4 months out of the season, without rescue medication usage. A standardized grass pollen challenge (at a stable count of 1450-1500 grains per m, with symptom evaluation every 15 min) was performed at baseline and after 1 week and 1, 2 and 4 months of treatment. The primary efficacy endpoint was the active versus placebo difference in the Average Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (ARTSS) after 4 months or at the last challenge. Allergen-specific basophil activation, T cell proliferation and plasma IgE and IgG responses were also assessed before and after 4 months of treatment. Results: In the ITT population (n = 89), a significant treatment effect was seen after the 1st month (P = 0.0042) and 2nd month (P = 0.02) of SLIT and was maintained through to month 4 (P = 0.0007). In the active group, the ARTSS decreased at each challenge, i.e. week 1 (7.40 ± 2.68), month 1 (5.89 ± 2.43), month 2 (5.09 ± 2.09) and month 4 (4.85 ± 2.0). The SLIT was well tolerated. The most frequent adverse events (>5% in at least one group) were local and as expected: oral pruritus, throat irritation and ear pruritus. Of the immunological parameters tested, only induction of allergen-specific IgGs was associated with a clinical response. Conclusion: For the first time ever, the onset of action and efficacy of Oralair®Grasses SLIT tablets have been assessed (versus placebo) in an ACC. The effect of this 300 IR grass pollen SLIT tablet on rhinoconjunctivitis symptoms was statistically significant (versus placebo) from the first month of treatment onward.
The improvement in disease specific quality of life is sustained 1 year after completion of 3 years treatment with the a grass allergen immunotherapy tablet; Background: Grass pollen induced allergic rhinoconjunctivitis affects 15-25% of the European population. Seventy percent of all allergic patients report that their condition significantly impairs their quality of life (QoL). Three years of all year round treatment with the ALK grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense 75 000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK-Abello), has previously been shown to reduce rhinoconjunctivitis symptoms and medication use. Importantly, this was accompanied by a significant improvement in the QoL of the patients. In this trial we investigated whether the improvement in QoL was sustained 1 year after completion of 3 years treatment. Methods: The trial was a parallel-group, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicentre trial in which patients were randomised to receive 3 years of either active treatment (n = 157) or placebo (n = 126), followed by 1 year of follow-up to investigate the long-term effects after treatment withdrawal. Disease specific QoL was assessed after the 1st year of follow-up based on Juniper's Rhinoconjunctivitis Quality of Life Questionnaire (RQLQ), which was completed by the patients weekly during the entire grass pollen season. The RQLQ defines QoL based on seven individual domains (activities, sleep, non-nose/eye symptoms, practical problems, nasal problems, eye problems, and emotional) assessed on a scale from 0-6 (0: 'not troubled', 6: 'extremely troubled'). Results: The overall RQLQ score (mean of the seven domains) for the entire season (average length = 65 days) was significantly improved in patients receiving the ALK grass AIT compared with placebo (23%, P = 0.004, see table). This improvement was even more pronounced during the peak grass pollen season (length = 15 days, difference = 28%, P = 0.001). All seven domains were improved in patients receiving active treatment (data not shown). Conclusion: These results demonstrate that the previously observed improvement of disease specific QoL in adult patients suffering from grass allergy receiving 3 years of daily all year round treatment with the ALK grass AIT is sustained 1 year following completion of treatment indicating a disease modifying effect of therapy.
An exciting view on the immunological compartment during specific immunotherapy with grass tablet treatment; Background: Fast-dissolving grass tablets (GRAZAX, ALK-Abello) are the first allergy sublingual tablet to address the underlying cause of grass pollen allergy. GRAZAX® is an once-daily sublingual immunotherapy tablet for home treatment administration. Three Grazax studies display a degree of efficacy almost identical to that found in a recent meta-analysis of 21 SLIT studies. The literature on clinical efficacy of SLIT shows discrepancies between IgG4 level and clinical efficacy and some authors suggest that immunoglobulin production could be an epiphenomenon reflecting the stimulation of a response, after a repeated administration of a foreign protein. The most important mechanism of positive response to SLIT is considered the switch from Th2 to Th1 pattern. Recent studies were performed on production of IL-10 and on its effects on T-cell function and B-cell switching to IgG. Recently, patients treated with SLIT showed an increased number of T-reg cell (CD25 bright, IL-10 producing cells), but it was not demonstrated if this is the only active mechanism of SLIT efficacy or the most important one. Study Aim: The present work looks at the immunological system in a more extensive way, in order to explain the efficacy of grass tablets. Both, humoral and cellular compartment have been evaluated. Patients and methods: Twenty grass allergic patients participating in the GT-17 study (a multicenter trial) were evaluated at Day 0, 14, 180 and 360 of grass tablet treatment. Furthermore, cellular compartment was analized by flow cytometry (FC 500 Instrumentation Laboratory
Grass allergen tablet immunotherapy in children induces IgG4 antibody and 4IgE-blocking responses similar to those seen in adults; Background: Serum antibody responses were investigated after ALK grass allergen tablet immunotherapy (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense 75 000 SQ-T/2800 BAU, ALK-Abelló) in children and results compared to those obtained after the same treatment in adults. Methods: In randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled multi-centre trials in children (5-16 year) and adults (18-65 year), AIT was initiated prior to the grass pollen season, and once-daily treatment was continued until the end of the season. All participants had a clinical history of moderate to severe grass allergy. The levels of Phleum pratense specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor (i.e. serum components competing with IgE for allergen binding) were assessed in serum samples before, during and after the grass pollen season. Results: In children, specific IgG4 was significantly increased from the initial level at all assessments after treatment initiation in the active group (P < 0.001). The increase after 7.3 months of treatment was 32 U/l. In adults, 8 months of treatment led to an increase in the active group of 45.7 U/l from the initial level (P < 0.001). The IgE-blocking factor increased in the active AIT groups in both children and adults (significantly different treatment effect in the active groups compared to the placebo groups, P < 0.001 in both trials). In children the difference from the initial level in IgE-blocking factor in the active group was 0.25 after 7.3 months, while in adults the difference was 0.15 after 8 months. The adult placebo group showed no significant change while the paediatric placebo group showed a slight increase in IgE-blocking factor during the grass pollen season. The clinical treatment effect in children by means of the rhinoconjunctivitis symptom score and the percentage of well days was also similar to that observed in adults when looking at comparable pollen exposure periods. Conclusion: These data confirm comparable elevations in serum IgG4 antibodies and IgE-blocking activity in children compared with adults following successful treatment with ALK grass AIT. In addition, the higher seasonal elevations in IgE-blocking activity in placebo-treated children compared with adults, suggest that children may establish a more tolerogenic rather than allergic immune response during the grass pollen season. This observation suggests that the use of AIT in childhood may ameliorate the ongoing natural course of the disease more efficiently than AIT during adulthood.

Efficacy of sublingual immunotherapy with a high-dose 6-grass pollen preparation progressively increases in the third treatment year; Background: Efficacy of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) depends on a sufficiently high maintenance dose as recently confirmed by the ARIA workshop report (2008 update). Often SLIT trials last for at most 2 years, and only few data are available about the effect of further treatment years. Progressive efficacy of a high-dose Single Dose grass pollen preparation has been shown in a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicentre trial for the first two treatment years. The preparation was standardized to the content of 40 μg grasses group 5 allergens in the maintenance dose. Aim of this study was to investigate the effect of a third treatment year. Methods: After a baseline period in 2003 grass pollen-allergic patients (rhinoconjunctivitis with or without asthma) received treatment with AllerSlit forte or placebo for two consecutive years (2004 and 2005). Data from 39 of the former 49 actively treated patients in the Main Data Set (MDS) were available in the third, open treatment year (2006). Symptom and medication scores, and the number of 'well days' (days with a symptom score ≤4 and without use of any rescue medication) were evaluated. Results: Symptom and medication scores were further improved during the third treatment year in comparison to the year before. The percentage of patients who did not need any rescue medication during the 42-day observation period in the grass pollen season continuously increased during the study: 0% at baseline, 19% at the end of the double-blind phase in 2005 to 31% in the third treatment year. Additionally, the median number of 'well days' increased during the 3 years: from 6/42 days at baseline pollen season, and 19.5/42 days after two treatment years in 2005 to 35/42 'well days' after 3 years of SLIT. The high-dose preparation was well tolerated with mainly mild adverse reactions. No serious drugrelated adverse events occurred. Conclusion: The third year of SLIT with the high-dose grass pollen preparation caused an additional benefit for the patients. Allergic symptoms and the intake of rescue medication were further reduced during the grass pollen season. The high number of 'well days' affirms the progressive efficacy.

Relation between grass pollen exposure and efficacy for a grass allergen immunotherapy tablet; Background: Pollen levels display extreme day-to-day variation. Hence, post hoc analyses exploring the effect of grass pollen exposure on efficacy measures and observed differences between treatment groups were performed for a phase III trial investigating the efficacy and safety in children of the once-daily ALK grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense, 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU). Methods: A total of 253 children aged 5-16 years with grass allergy were randomised 1 : 1 to receive either active treatment or placebo prior to and throughout the grass pollen season 2007. Symptomatic medications were provided as rescue medication to both groups in a step-wise fashion. Statistical analyses of efficacy parameters were performed with two different definitions of the pollen season, using 10 or 30 grains/m3 as threshold. In addition, the effect of pollen exposure was explored by investigating the efficacy for days with low, medium and high pollen counts and by modelling the dependence of the efficacy scores on the pollen count for each treatment group. Results: Statistically significant differences between active and placebo demonstrated effect of the ALK grass AIT throughout the entire grass pollen season for both pollen season definitions. The definition of the pollen season influenced the efficacy outcome. Using 10 grains/m3, the relative differences between active and placebo were 22% for both the mean symptom score and for well days. These differences increased upon replacing the threshold with 30 grains/m3, to 28% for the symptom score and 44% for well days. On days with high pollen counts (>50 grains/m3), symptoms increased compared to days with low pollen counts (≤10 grains/m3), from a daily average of 2.1 to 3.8 for the placebo group and from 1.7 to 2.7 for the active group. Simultaneously the average medication score increased from 0.8 to 2.4 for the placebo group but only from 0.4 to 0.9 for the active group. Modelling of the efficacy scores showed a substantial increase in the difference between active and placebo upon increasing pollen load. Conclusion: This was the first phase III trial investigating the ALK grass AIT in children. A clear statistically significant difference between active and placebo was seen throughout the entire grass pollen season, in favour of active treatment. On days with high pollen counts the clinical benefit of the ALK grass AIT was markedly increased, both for symptoms and for the use of symptomatic medication.
Treatment with a grass allergen immunotherapy tablet in children above and below the age of 10 - efficacy, safety and immunological response; Background: Treatment with the ALK grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense, 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU), is efficacious and well-tolerated in adult grass-allergic patients. Younger children are often perceived as good candidates for immunotherapy, and post hoc analyses to explore possible differences between younger and older children were performed for an ALK grass AIT paediatric phase III trial. Methods: A total of 253 children aged 5-16 years with grass allergy were randomised 1 : 1 to receive either active treatment or placebo prior to and throughout the grass pollen season 2007. Symptomatic medications were provided as rescue medication to both groups. The population was divided into two age groups comprising 140 children aged 5-10 years and 113 children aged 11-16 years. The effect of age on efficacy was also analysed by including age as a covariate in the symptom score analysis. Results: Including age as a covariate in the symptom score analysis indicated age to have no influence on the efficacy, as demonstrated by a P-value of 0.6 for the interaction between age and treatment. Analysis of the difference between active and placebo for each age group showed a slightly higher relative difference in symptom score for the younger children compared with the older, 26% versus 19%. The difference between active and placebo was statistically significant for the 5-10 year old (P = 0.047) although the trial was powered for analysis of the population as a whole. In the active group the percentage of adverse events (AEs) assessed as treatment-related was nominally lower for the younger group than for the older children, 32% versus 40%. The percentages of AEs assessed as moderate or severe in the active group were similar for the two age groups. For both active and placebo, the overall number of AEs was nominally higher in the younger group than in the older group. The immunological response (IgE, IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor) was similar for the two age groups. There was a tendency for the younger group towards an increased response in IgE-blocking factor with onset of the grass pollen season, evident in both the actively treated and the placebo group. Conclusion: This was the first phase III trial investigating the ALK grass AIT in children. Once-daily administration was found to be clinically efficacious and welltolerated. The effect was comparable for children below and above the age of 10, as was the safety pattern and the immunological response.

In-season initiation of treatment with SQ standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet induces an immunomodulatory response and seems to be well tolerated; Background: Clinical efficacy and a favourable safety profile of the SQ standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet (AIT, Grazax®, Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T, ALK-Abelló) have been demonstrated in several trials. The current recommendation is to start treatment 4 months prior to the grass pollen season and to continue treatment for 3 consecutive years. A large proportion of allergy patients have their first visit to the physician during the pollen season, i.e. when they experience their symptoms. Currently, these subjects are offered symptomatic therapy and the opportunity to start immunotherapy after the pollen season. For these patients, it would be of interest to be able to start treatment in the season. Therefore, in the present trial the pharmacodynamic effect and tolerability of in-season initiation of AIT treatment were evaluated. Methods: The trial was a parallel-group, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicentre trial conducted in 28 centres in Germany and Austria. A total of 276 subjects with grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis with or without asthma were randomised 4 : 1 to receive either active (N = 219) or placebo (N = 57) once daily, starting during the grass pollen season and continuing at least 8 weeks (+ 1-2 weeks). Pharmacodynamic endpoints included the change from baseline of IgE-blocking factor, IgE, and IgG4. Tolerability assessment was based on adverse events. Results: An immunomodulatory effect of in-season treatment was demonstrated with statistically significant higher inductions of IgE-blocking factor (P < 0.0001), IgE (P < 0.0001) and IgG4 (P <.0001) in the active group as compared with placebo. Overall, in-season treatment was well tolerated and comparable to what has been observed in other clinical trials in which treatment was initiated outside the pollen season. Most of the reported adverse events were mild or moderate in severity. The majority of events related to treatment were local reactions, mainly oral pruritus. Four serious adverse events, all considered unlikely related to treatment, were reported during the trial. No safety concerns were observed regarding FEV1, vital sign or physical examinations. Conclusion: In-season initiation of treatment with the grass AIT induces an immunomodulatory response in subjects with grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis. Furthermore, in-season initiation was found to be well tolerated, however a suitable powered trial would be required to confirm the safety profile.

Implementation of pre-seasonal sublingual immunotherapy with a five-grass pollen tablet during optimal dosage assessment; Background: The optimal dose (300IR) of a five-grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy tablet in terms of efficacy was previously demonstrated from the first pollen season. Objective: Here, we aim to confirm whether this dose remained optimal during the peak of the pollen season by assessing the efficacy and quality of life data. Methods: A total of 628 subjects with grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis were randomized in a double-blind, placebo-controlled, multi-centre, pan-European trial. Subjects received once-daily tablets (Stallergenes, Antony, France) of 100IR, 300IR, 500IR or placebo, starting 4 months before and throughout the 2005 grass pollen season. The pollen season was defined as the first day of 3 consecutive days with a grass pollen count above 30 grains/m3 of air, recorded using Hirst-type volumetric pollen traps, to the last day before 3 consecutive days with a pollen count below 30 grains/m3. Results: The grass pollen season lasted an average of 30 days, with a peak of 12 days. The mean treatment duration before the grass pollen season was similar in the four treatment groups (121.4±31.1 to 128.6±15.4 days in the safety population). Both the 300IR and 500IR groups had highly significant improvements in Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (RTSS) vs. placebo at the peak pollen season (P=0.0005 and 0.0014, respectively), which agreed with improvements in RTSS in the primary evaluations. The average RTSS scores were slightly elevated during the peak pollen season in all treatment groups. The overall Rhinoconjunctivitis Quality of Life Questionnaire score confirmed the optimal dosage 300IR at peak (P<0.0001) and at the end (P≤0.0031) of the pollen season. All doses were well tolerated. Conclusion: At the peak pollen season, the efficacy and quality of life data for both 300IR and 500IR groups was significantly improved vs. the placebo group. These results confirm the conclusions of the primary evaluations and validate the use of 300IR tablets for clinical practice. © 2008 Stallergenes.
Efficacy and safety of high-doses sublingual immunotherapy in ultra-rush scheme in children allergic to grass pollen; Background: Although sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) has been used with increasing frequency, the data on the efficacy of SLIT in pediatric asthma are limited. Aim The aim of our study was to evaluate the efficacy and the safety of high-dose SLIT given pre-seasonally and co-seasonally in an ultra-rush scheme in children with bronchial asthma allergic to grass pollen. Methods: Fifty children with asthma, aged 6-17, sensitive to grass pollen, participated in the 2-year prospective, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial, to investigate the efficacy and safety of SLIT (Staloral 300 IR, Stallergenes SA, 25 μg major allergens) as a standardized extract of five grass pollen with ultra-rush induction. Results: SLIT significantly improved asthma symptom scores (41% vs. placebo group), reduced nasal symptoms (25% vs. placebo group) and the use of rescue medications (10% vs. placebo group), improved forced expiratory volume in 1 s, but had no effect on ocular symptoms, nasal hyper-reactivity, peak expiratory flow and forced expiratory volume between 25% and 75% of vital capacity. Serum levels of immunoglobulin E and IgG4 did not change after SLIT. After the second season of SLIT, an improvement in bronchial hyperresponsiveness was observed
Evaluation of a compliance device in a subgroup of adult patients receiving specific immunotherapy with grass allergen tablets (GRAZAX®) in a randomized, open-label, controlled study: An a priori subgroup analysis; Objectives: This a priori subgroup analysis was conducted to assess patients' experience with a compliance device for the administration of sublingual specific immunotherapy for grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis. Methods: The present paper reports the results of a subgroup analysis of a multicenter, randomized, controlled, open-label European study in which adults with grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis received once-daily treatment with grass allergen tablets with or without a device to aid compliance. Treatment was given ~6 to 12 weeks before the grass pollen season, during the season (~8-10 weeks), and for up to 2 weeks after the end of the season. In the subgroup analysis, patients from Germany and the Netherlands who had been randomized to receive the compliance device were asked to complete a brief questionnaire at the final study visit. The questionnaire included 6 items concerning patients' use of the device and whether it helped them remember to take their tablets. Results: Of the 91 patients from Germany and the Netherlands who were randomized to use the compliance device along with grass allergen tablets, 71 returned the questionnaire. Among responders, 58 of 71 (82%) reported using the device sometimes or always, 50 of 63 (79%) found the device easy to use, 32 of 69 (46%) found that the device made it easier to remember to take tablets, and 43 of 71 (61%) indicated that they would consider using the device again. Conclusion: Most patients in this subgroup analysis used the compliance device as a medication reminder and rated it easy to use. © 2009 Excerpta Medica Inc. All rights reserved.
Agreement of efficacy assessments for five grass pollen Sublingual Immunotherapy (SLIT) tablets in children and adolescents with grass pollen Rhinoconjunctivitis and with or without mild asthma; RATIONALE: The efficacy and safety of five-grass pollen 300 IR SLITtablets in the treatment of rhinoconjunctivitis in children have been recently demonstrated. Secondary analyses on the trial's intention-to-treat (ITT) population (n = 266) were performed concerning the average Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (ARTSS) and the six Average Individual Symptom Scores (AISSs) and according to age class (5 to 11 and 12 to 17) and asthma status. METHODS: Children were included in a Phase III, randomized, doubleblind, placebo-controlled, multicentre trial and were treated 4 months prior to and throughout the pollen season. Each symptom was evaluated daily by the patients (with parental assistance, for the youngest) on a four-point scale from 0 (absent) to 3 (severe). Initial asthma status was recorded during a screening interview and physical examination. RESULTS: The difference between the 300 IR and placebo tablets was significant for rhinorrhea (p=0.038), nasal congestion (p=0.012), ocular pruritus (p < 0.0001) and watery eyes (p=0.0008). for sneezing and nasal pruritus, improvements but non-significant were noted. In both the active and placebo groups, the ARTSS and the six AISSs were similar for patients with asthma (n = 28 and n = 29, respectively) and without asthma (n = 103 and n = 106, respectively). In both age classes, the ARTSS was lower in the 300 IR group (2.94 and 3.77, respectively) than in the placebo group (4.08 and 4.98, respectively). CONCLUSIONS: These secondary analyses confirm that the 300 IR dose of SLIT pollen tablet is effective in the treatment of grass pollen allergic rhinoconjunctivitis in children, independently of age class and asthma status.

Evaluation of a five grass-pollen Sublingual Immunotherapy Tablets (SLIT) in a pediatric population at peak pollen season and immunological outcomes; RATIONALE: The efficacy and safety of grass-pollen SLIT tablets in children (aged 5 to 17) have been established using the Average Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (ARTSS) as a primary endpoint, in a single-season, double-blind, placebo controlled Phase III study. Secondary and exploratory analyses, such as the ARTSS improvement at the pollen peak and levels of immunological markers are presented. METHODS: 278 children, with grass-pollen rhinoconjunctivitis were randomized to receive daily either a 300 IR SLIT tablets or placebo starting 4 months before the pollen season and throughout the latter. The peak pollen period was defined as the 10-14 consecutive days with the highest mean pollen counts. Immunological markers and skin prick tests (SPTs) were evaluated before and after treatment RESULTS: Between groups, there was a highly significant (p = 0.0009) ARTSS difference. At the pollen peak, the improvement versus placebo was 28% (mean) and 43% (median). The geometric mean IgG4 level (μg/ L) more than tripled from baseline to endpoint for children receiving 300IR (ratio 3.37) but did not change in the placebo group (ratio 1.41). In contrast, the geometric mean IgE level (kU/L) was similar at baseline and endpoint for 300IR (ratio 1.35), but slightly higher post-treatment for placebo (ratio1.64). Concerning the SPTs, patients on 300IR had a larger mean decrease in wheal diameter (-1.73 mm) than those on placebo (-0.77 mm). CONCLUSIONS: As for the entire pollen season, the efficacy of 300 IR SLIT grass pollen tablets was confirmed for the pollen peak. In children receiving SLIT, specific IgG4 levels clearly increased while specific IgE levels remained stable.
The response to sublingual immunotherapy with grass pollen extract administered as a single extract or as part of a multi-allergen extract in patients with seasonal allergic rhinitis caused by grass pollen; RATIONALE: The purpose of this study was to examine whether the efficacy of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) with standardized timothy extract alone was reduced by combination with other allergen extracts. METHODS: A single-center, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trialwith SLIT was conducted. After an observational grass season, SLIT was administered to 54 patients for 10 months. The subjects were randomized to 1 of 3 treatment arms: placebo, timothy extract (20 mcg Phl p = daily) as monotherapy (TM), or the same dose of timothy extract plus 9 additional allergen extracts (multi-allergen therapy, MAT). Compliance was >80%. Symptom and medication scores, titrated nasal challenges (NC), titrated skin prick tests (tSPT), specific IgE and IgG4 to timothy were measured. PBMC swere stimulated with timothy allergen to measure secreted cytokines. RESULTS: There was no significant difference in medication or symptom scores in either treatment groups compared to placebo.There was a significant improvement in the TMNC results verses placebo (p = 0.03).There were also significant improvements in tSP Tin the TM group (p = 0.001) and MAT (p = 0.037) compared to placebo but not between TM and MAT. IFN- γ levels significantly decreased in the TM verses placebo groups (p = 0.02). Therewere significantlymore adverse events in the active groups, TM (84%, p < 0.0001) and MAT (65%, p = 0.0003), compared to placebo (6%). CONCLUSIONS: Although there were no significant outcome differences between TM and MAT, trends for greater improvement in several parameters with TM suggest a reduction in SLIT efficacy with multi-allergen treatment.
Sublingual immunotherapy with fast-dissolving grass tablets induces comparable IgG4 antibody and IgE-blocking responses in children and adults; RATIONALE: Serum antibody responses after treatment with sublingual ALK grass tablet (GRAZAX®, Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK-Abelló) in children and adults were investigated to compare treatment effects. METHODS: In randomised, parallel-group, double-blind, placebo-controlled multi-centre trials in adults (18y-65y) and children (5y-16y), sublingual immunotherapy with ALK grass tablet was initiated prior to the grass pollen season and once-dailytreatmentwas continued until the end of the season. The levels of Phleum pratense specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor were assessed in serum samples before, during and after the pollen season. RESULTS: In children, specific IgG4 was significantly increased from the initial level at all assessments after treatment initiation in the active group (p < 0.001). The increase after 7.3 months of treatment was 32 U/L. In adults, 8 months of treatment led to an increase from the initial level of 49.5 U/L in the active group (p < 0.001). The IgE-blocking factor (serumcomponents competing with IgE for allergen binding) increased in both active groups (significantly different treatment effect in the active groups compared to the placebo groups, p < 0.001 in both trials). In children the difference fromthe initial level in the active group was 0.25 after 7.3 months, while in adults the differencewas 0.18 after 8 months. The adult placebo group showed no change while the paediatric placebo group showed a slight increase during the grass pollen season. CONCLUSIONS: Immunoreactive IgG4 and IgE-blocking activity found in a paediatric population following sublingual immunotherapy with fastdissolving grass tablets closely resembled the corresponding humoral immune responses found in adults.
Study of sublingual immunotherapy in subjects with dermatophagoides farniae and timothy grass allergy; RATIONALE: We are currently investigating the safety of concurrent Dermatophagoides farinae (DF) and Timothy Grass (TG) sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) in patients ages 5-55. T cell phenotyping and plasma cytokine profiles will be analyzed to investigate the development of tolerance in treated subjects, reducing the severity of allergic rhinitis (AR) and allergic conjunctivitis (AC) symptoms. METHODS: This is a double-blind 2:1 randomized placebo-controlled study in thirty subjects with multiple allergies including at least DF and TG affected with allergic rhinoconjunctivitis with or without atopic asthma. Initial enrollment began with 5 subjects dosed prior to spring grass pollen season. Paired plasma samples from these initial patients were analyzed at start of therapy compared 2 months after dosing using Luminex 35 plex studies and ELISA. RESULTS: Phenotyping of T cell subsets and levels of plasma markers demonstrated an increase in tolerogenic markers including IL-10 (5.3 fold), and TGB beta (9.5 fold), and XCL1(3:1fold) in 3 of 5 subjects, after 2 months of dosing. In contrast, decreases were seen in IL-4, IL-13 and IL-9, inflammatory cytokines, by at least 4 fold. CONCLUSIONS: This is the first double allergen SLIT trial in children, to study specific T cell down regulation to allergens as well as non specific tolerance markers. The response to immunotherapy with SLIT may induce regulatory T cell up regulation with decreases of the IgE response to DF and TG. We will determine if subjects have a 30% improvement in their symptom scores for AR/AC since receiving SLIT especially during the grass pollen seasons over the next two years.
Agreement of efficacy assessments for five-grass pollen sublingual tablet immunotherapy; Background: The optimal dose of five-grass pollen sublingual tablet immunotherapy (SLIT) was established recently by the primary criteria Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (RTSS) from the first treatment season. Secondary and exploratory criteria, such as RTSS at peak pollen season, exploratory combined symptom and rescue medication use score, quality of life and immunological markers are calculated and described in this analysis. Methods: Six hundred and twenty-eight patients with grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis (≥2 years duration) were randomized in a double-blind, placebo-controlled trial conducted in Europe. Patients received once-daily SLIT (Stallergenes, Antony, France) of 100IR, 300IR, 500IR or placebo, starting 4 months before grass pollen season and throughout the 2005 season. Patients were instructed to take rescue medication only if symptoms were severe and record symptom severity on using the RTSS. Results: Both 300IR and 500IR doses significantly reduced mean RTSS at pollen peak (P = 0.0005 and P = 0.0014, respectively) and the exploratory combined score (P = 0.0001 and P = 0.0026, respectively) compared with placebo. Compared with patients in the placebo group, those who were taking the 300IR and 500IR doses reported significantly improved quality of life using the mean Rhinoconjunctivitis Quality of Life Questionnaire scores during the peak of the pollen season (P < 0.0001) and at the end of the pollen season (P = 0.0031 and P ≤ 0.0001, respectively). Specific immunoglobulin G4 increased significantly depending on the SLIT dose (P < 0.0001). Conclusions: All secondary efficacy criteria, including efficacy at pollen peak, combined score, quality of life and immunological changes, indicate that 300IR tablets represent the optimal dose and suggest it is appropriate for use in clinical practice. © 2008 The Authors.
Efficacy of grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy for three consecutive seasons and after cessation of treatment: The ECRIT study; Background: Data supporting a carry-over effect with sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) are scarce. This randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study evaluated the efficacy, carry-over effect and safety of grass pollen SLIT using co-seasonal treatment. Methods: Patients (7.9-64.7 years) with grass pollen allergy received ultra-rush titration with increasing doses (30, 90, 150 and 300 IR) of a 5-grass pollen mixture every 20 min at the start of the pollen seasons, followed by 300 IR daily until the end of the pollen seasons. A baseline season (no SLIT) was followed by three consecutive treatment seasons and one follow-up season. Symptoms, medication and adverse events were documented and specific immunoglobulin (Ig)E and IgG4 measured. Results: Data were analysed for 183 of the 213 randomized patients. Mean treatment duration varied between seasons (81.8-92.7 days). Combined scores (symptoms and medication) improved progressively across treatment seasons (up to 44.7% improvement for SLIT compared with baseline) and fluctuated between -11.3% and -14.8% for placebo (P < 0.05). Similar changes were observed for symptom scores, with a successive decrease of 39.7% (SLIT) and fluctuations between +13.6% and -1.51% for placebo (P < 0.05). Combined score (P = 0.0508) and symptom score improvements (P = 0.0144) with SLIT continued during follow up. Increases in specific IgG4 observed in the first season were sustained for SLIT vs placebo throughout treatment (P = 0.0001). Titration and daily SLIT were well tolerated. No serious systemic or anaphylactic reactions were reported. Conclusions: Seasonal SLIT with ultra-rush titration is well tolerated and effective from the first treatment season onwards. These data indicate a carry-over effect of seasonal SLIT. © 2008 The Authors.
Safety and efficacy in children of an SQ-standardized grass allergen tablet for sublingual immunotherapy; Background: Immunotherapy with the SQ-standardized grass tablet Grazax is efficacious and well-tolerated in adult patients with rhinoconjunctivitis. Allergic asthma and rhinoconjunctivitis are closely linked, and a strategy combining treatment of the upper and lower airways is recommended. Objective: To investigate the efficacy of treatment with the grass tablet on grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis and asthma as well as the immunologic response and the safety profile in children. Methods: A total of 253 children age 5 to 16 years, with grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis with/without asthma, were randomized 1:1 to active treatment or placebo. Treatment was initiated 8 to 23 weeks before the start of the grass pollen season 2007 and continued throughout the entire season. Symptomatic medication was provided as relief medication to both groups in a stepwise fashion. Primary endpoints were rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores. Results: The rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores and the asthma symptom score were all statistically significantly different between the 2 treatment groups. The differences in medians relative to placebo were 24%, 34%, and 64% in favor of active treatment. The immunologic response was similar to that observed in adults. The most common adverse reaction was oral pruritus, reported by 40 subjects (32%) in the active and 3 (2%) in the placebo group. Six subjects withdrew because of adverse events. No serious adverse events were assessed as treatment-related. Conclusion: Immunotherapy with the grass tablet reduced grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis and asthma symptoms in a pediatric population and introduced an immunomodulatory response, consistent with treatment of the underlying allergic disease. The treatment was well tolerated. © 2009 American Academy of Allergy, Asthma & Immunology.

Inflammation in respiratory allergy treated by sublingual immunotherapy; The most common allergic diseases, such as rhinitis, asthma and atopic dermatitis, are sustained by allergic inflammation, the treatment of which requires anti-inflammatory activity. Among the available treatments, allergen immunotherapy (IT) has a documented impact on allergic inflammation which persists after its discontinuation and modifies the natural course of allergy. The anti-inflammatory effects of IT, and particularly of sublingual IT (SLIT), are based on the ability to modify the phenotype of T cells which, in allergic subjects, are characterized by a prevalence of the Th2 type, with production of IL-4, IL-5, IL-13, IL-17, and IL-32 cytokines. IT-induced changes result in a Th1-type response (immune deviation) related to an increased IFN-gamma and IL-2 production or in a Th2 reduced activity, through a mechanism of anergy or tolerance. It is now known that T cell tolerance is characterized by the generation of allergen-specific Treg cells, which produce cytokines such as IL-10 and TGF-beta with immunosuppressant and/or immunoregulatory activity. Recent studies suggest that the anti-inflammatory mechanism of SLIT is similar to classical, subcutaneous IT, with a prominent role in SLIT for mucosal dendritic cells. The tolerance pattern induced by Treg accounts for the suppressed or reduced activity of inflammatory cells and for the isotypic switch of antibody synthesis from IgE to IgG, and especially to IgG4. Data obtained from biopsies clearly indicate that the pathophysiology of the oral mucosa plays a pivotal role in inducing tolerance to the sublingually administered allergen. Copyright © by BIOLIFE, s.a.s.
Allergen immunotherapy in childhood; Allergen-specific imunotherapy (SIT) is the only treatment that interferes with basic pathophysiological mechanisms of allergic disease. New data demonstrates a role of SIT not only as a therapeutic agent but also as a preventive strategy to reduce onset of new sensitization and progression from allergic rhinitis to asthma. SIT is the only treatment that affects the natural course of the allergic disease and therefore should be started early in childhood. Data of SIT in children under the age of 5 is rare however. There is a need for studies investigating the primary and secondary preventive effect in very young children. Future research also needs to focus on treatment of atopic dermatitis and food allergy with SIT. Sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) is a promising new modality of SIT since it has a good safety profile and is patient-friendly. The results of paediatric SLIT studies are heterogeneous and meta-analyses do not allow general recommendations for SLIT in childhood. New data however demonstrates that high dose of grass allergen tablets in children is capable to reduce symptom and medication scores in a same magnitude as observed in adults previously. New SLIT studies using high dose of other seasonal and perennial allergens as well as trials in children with asthma are required in order to define indications for SLIT in childhood. Furthermore, follow-up trials are needed to further elucidate the long-term effect and preventive capacity of SLIT in children. © 2009 Dustri-Verlag Dr. Karl Feistle.
Efficacy and safety of 5-grass-pollen sublingual immunotherapy tablets in pediatric allergic rhinoconjunctivitis; Background: The efficacy and safety of the 300-index of reactivity (IR) dose of 5-grass-pollen sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) tablets (Stallergènes, Antony, France) have been demonstrated for the treatment of hay fever in adults. Objective: We sought to assess the efficacy and safety of this tablet in children and adolescents with grass pollen-related allergic rhinitis. Methods: In this multinational, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study, 278 children (5-17 years of age) with grass pollen-related rhinoconjunctivitis (confirmed by means of a positive grass pollen skin prick test response and serum-specific IgE measurement) received once-daily SLIT tablets or placebo. Treatment was initiated 4 months before the estimated pollen season and continued throughout the season. The primary outcome was the rhinoconjunctivitis total symptom score (RTSS), a sum of 6 individual symptom scores: sneezing, runny nose, itchy nose, nasal congestion, watery eyes, and itchy eyes. Secondary end points included rescue medication intake, individual scores, and safety. Results: The intent-to-treat population included 266 children (mean age, 10.9 ± 3.22 years). The RTSS for the 300-IR group was highly significantly different from that of the placebo group (P = .001). The 300-IR group showed a mean improvement for the RTSS of 28.0% over that seen with placebo and a median improvement of 39.3%. Significant differences between the 300-IR and placebo groups were also observed regarding rescue medication score and proportion of days using rescue medication during the pollen season (P = .0064 and P = .0146, respectively). Adverse events were generally mild or moderate in intensity and expected. No serious side effects were reported. Conclusion: Five-grass-pollen SLIT tablets (300 IR) reduce both symptom scores and rescue medication use in children and adolescents with grass pollen-related rhinoconjunctivitis. © 2009 American Academy of Allergy, Asthma & Immunology.

Both groups had access to symptomatic medication
Sublingual immunotherapy: What have we learnt from the 'big trials'?; Purpose of review: Recent systematic reviews support the use of sublingual immunotherapy for allergic rhinoconjunctivitis in adults, whereas data in children have been less convincing. The present review evaluates three recent 'definitive' trials in adults and one in children. Recent findings: Two large independent randomized controlled trials of grass allergen tablets have confirmed the efficacy of sublingual immunotherapy in adults with seasonal allergic rhinoconjunctivitis. Effects were both allergen dose-dependent and time-dependent. Tablets were well tolerated and equally effective in monosensitized compared with polysensitized patients and in patients with peak seasonal asthma (patients with perennial asthma were specifically excluded). Local side effects were common but largely self-limiting and not bothersome. There were no serious treatment-related adverse events. Results were similar in magnitude to those observed in a comparable study of subcutaneous immunotherapy using an alum-based vaccine. A trial of sublingual drops in children with hayfever in a primary care setting was negative, although these results could not be generalized. Summary: Sublingual immunotherapy represents an effective and well tolerated treatment for seasonal allergic rhinoconjunctivitis in adults. Current ongoing paediatric trials and evaluation of long-term effects in adults will further define its role in therapy. © 2008 Wolters Kluwer Health | Lippincott Williams & Wilkins.
Subcutaneous and sublingual immunotherapy in children: complete update on controversies, dosing, and efficacy.; For this review, articles on immunotherapy dosing in pediatric respiratory allergy were identified via PubMed, through congressional abstracts for 2008, in reference lists of recent review articles, and via personal communication with experts. In pediatric subcutaneous immunotherapy (SCIT), doses shown to be effective, mostly in aluminium-adsorbed preparations administered every 6 weeks, contain 20 microg of group 5 major allergen, 12 microg Bet v 1, 15 microg Fel d 1, and 5 to 20 microg Der p 1. Evidence indicates that SCIT prevents new sensitizations and asthma onset 7 years after discontinuation and reduces symptoms 12 years after a 5-year SCIT course, even though skin reactivity returns. Consistent evidence of effect exists for sublingual immunotherapy in pediatric respiratory allergy with daily 15- to 25-microg grass group 5 major allergen and 6 microg Bet v 1. Der p/f doses of 0.8/0.4 microg three times weekly (up to 27/57 microg daily) demonstrate inconsistent findings. Evidence of effect exists for SCIT in pediatric allergic rhinitis and asthma as treatment and preventive management. Evidence of effect exists for sublingual immunotherapy in pediatric allergic rhinoconjunctivitis and seasonal asthma. Similar results are doubtful for perennial asthma.
Sublingual immunotherapy in youngsters: Adherence in a randomized clinical trial; Background: Adherence is essential for effective treatment. Although several trials on the efficacy of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) in youngsters have been published, few contain data on medication intake. Objective: We aimed to quantify adherence both to study protocol and medication intake as well as to identify factors that may influence adherence to SLIT in youngsters with rhinoconjunctivitis. Methods: Two hundred and four youngsters (6-18 years) with hayfever participated in a randomized controlled trial and used grass pollen extract or placebo for 2 years. The primary outcome of the trial was the mean daily total rhinoconjunctivitis symptom score in the second grass pollen season. Participants having completed the follow-up were considered adherent to the study protocol. Adherence to medication intake was assessed by weighing the study medication. Participants who completed the follow-up and used ≥80% of the prescribed medication were considered adherent to medication intake. Patient-, disease- and treatment-related factors were analysed. Results: One hundred and fifty-four youngsters completed the study. The main reason for discontinuation was the inability to take medication according to schedule. Drop-outs were older, had more difficulty following the medication instructions and their overall evaluation of the treatment effect was lower. The number and reasons for drop-out did not differ between treatment groups. In total, 77% of the participants was adherent to medication intake. Self-reported adherence was 99%. Non-adherent participants experienced more severe symptoms before the trial. Symptom scores did not differ between adherent and non-adherent participants. In adherent as well as non-adherent participants, no difference was found between verum and placebo group with respect to symptom scores. Conclusion: Adherence to both study protocol and medication intake was good. Drop-out was affected by age, evaluation of the treatment effect and medication instructions. Non-adherence to medication intake was influenced by the severity of the disease before the trial. The ineffectiveness of SLIT could not be explained by non-adherence. © 2008 The Authors.
